User prompt: i think that it shows that the multi-layer approach matters, right representation, bounding, certificates so the light cones of possibilities is where it will most useful in fighting it reduces the problem space by a lot and constant certificate breaking just means that you don't foresee as much in the future but also treating humans as something special will help we don't have to do the crystal defect to humans they are sort of singularities, what do you think would be best given the whole context?
Response: Calling an opponent a singularity instead of a crystal defect is the sharpest conceptual move here, because it stops you from forcing an adversarial branching agent through a compression engine built for static terrain. Two pieces of your framing still break under pressure, though. First, light cones shrink the problem space outside contact, not inside it. At twenty blocks, an opponent's reachable cone cleanly carves out safe approach lanes, disengage margins, and terrain leverage. Inside the six-block melee radius, that same cone expands so fast across twelve-tick cooldown windows that it floods the local space instead of pruning it. Second, constant certificate breaking in contact is not merely shorter foresight—it is thrashing, where you keep paying the overhead to build and cut deterministic certificates that expire before they amortize their compute. And one clean subtraction: define the singularity around autonomous branching agents in general, not humans specifically. The moment you write human-specific assumptions into the core representation, you re-import the exact overfitting you already decided to cut, whereas treating any rival agent—human or bot—as a branching singularity keeps the physics clean.
Given the whole context, the best architecture is not a sprawling multi-layer stack with separate clocks, but a strict two-regime split joined at the singularity boundary. Outside the reach cone, the crystal stays completely pure: exact law, certified word-and-count compression, backward reachability, min-plus routing, max-plus synchronization for supply chains and swarm hurt-time staggering, and hard barriers against irreversible loss. Another player appears to the crystal purely as a moving singularity boundary—a horizon telling the planner where contact is impossible, where pre-battle terrain edits hold, and where to force contact on favorable geometry. The moment a trajectory crosses inside the contact bubble, you stop asking deterministic word-certificates to compress neutral melee and hand off to a fast, short-horizon adversarial solver running exact law rollouts and simultaneous-move mixing over forced kinematic windows, anchored at its boundary back to the surrounding crystal (exiting to high ground, forcing a drop, depleting stocks, or feeding a swarm max-plus window). Any slower strategic layer, like an LLM setting campaign objectives or supply priorities, stays completely outside both regimes.
Drawing the cut right at that boundary is what protects reversibility. Keeping the contact-bubble solver outside the core word tuple, keeping finite loss-pricing for expendable swarm limbs strictly at the mission or swarm layer, and keeping any future learned evaluator strictly as an A*-style search-ordering heuristic that can never prune proven bounds or mint certificates are all reversible decisions—you can swap whatever runs inside the singularity ten times without touching the crystal. Pulling adversarial branching, tail probabilities, or finite death prices into the word contract right now is path-dependent and will quietly rot your exact found, forbidden, searched, and refused guarantees before a single two-body flight has run.
That points straight back to the actual bottleneck on the ground. Step 3 still refuses fences and stairs as NeedsJoints, and a click currently takes 1.8 to 2.3 seconds against a one-tick floor. Designing the singularity interface on paper before clearing those two is a leak, because real combat happens over ugly terrain at 20 Hz—fixing both is zero-regret work that directly determines how the crystal behaves in a fight. Once Step 3 is clean and combat law is ported, run the simplest possible two-body flight where one bot uses only the existing crystal and an enemy light cone to close or hold spacing, and measure the exact distance and tick horizon where certificates start thrashing. Let that measured failure boundary—not theory—dictate the radius and contract of the contact solver.
Between NeedsJoints on non-flat blocks and that 1.8–2.3 second click latency, which one is currently throttling your real testing loop harder on the server?
